Parabolic induction in the homotopy category of pro- p p Iwahori-Hecke modules
Nicolas Dupré

Abstract
. Let G G be the group of rational points of a split connected reductive group over a non-archimedean local field of residue characteristic p p , and let ℋ {\mathcal{H}} denote the pro- p p Iwahori-Hecke algebra of G G over a field of characteristic p p . We study the parabolic induction functor for ℋ {\mathcal{H}} -modules in terms of the Gorenstein projective model structures introduced by Hovey. Let Ho ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}) denote the associated homotopy category of this model structure. We show that Ho ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}) and its thick subcategory generated by the essential images of finitely many parabolic induction functors are related via a recollement of triangulated categories. We then investigate the isomorphism classes of simple ℋ {\mathcal{H}} -modules in Ho ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}) and give a complete classification for simple supersingulars when G = GL n G=\mathrm{GL}_{n} .



†
†
footnotetext:
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
. Primary 20C08, 18N40, 18N55.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

Let
p
be a prime and let
\mathfrak{F}
be a non-archimedean local field of residue characteristic
p
. Let
G=\mathbb{G}(\mathfrak{F})
be the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-rational points of a split connected reductive group
\mathbb{G}
over
\mathfrak{F}
. Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p
and denote by
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
the category of
k
-linear smooth representations of
G
, i.e. those representations such that the stabiliser of each vector is open. This category lies on one side of the mod-
p
local Langlands program.

Let
I
be a pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup of
G
and let
{\mathcal{H}}=k[I\backslash G/I]
be the corresponding
pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebra
of
G
, i.e. the convolution algebra of
I
-biinvariant compactly supported functions on
G
. Write
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
for the category of right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. Then taking
I
-invariants defines a functor
U:{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
which sends non-zero representations to non-zero modules. This functor is expected to provide a strong relation between the two categories, however its behaviour is not sufficiently understood beyond
G=\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and a few related cases (cf. the work of Ollivier
[
24
]
). In particular, one of the major open problems in the theory is to determine the relationship between the isomorphism classes of irreducible admissible smooth representations of
G
and of simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules.

In previous work
[
9
]
with Kohlhaase, we showed that
U
restricts to an equivalence between an explicit full subcategory of
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
and the full subcategory
\mathrm{GProj}({\mathcal{H}})
of
Gorenstein projective

{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. These modules are a key component in the definition of a model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
due to Hovey
[
15
]
. We used this in
[
9
]
to construct and study a model structure on
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
and the derived functor
\mathbf{R}U
between the associated homotopy categories. We generally obtained that that the formal properties of this functor are improved at the homotopy level, for instance
\mathbf{R}U
is essentially surjective. This suggests that one should instead investigate the relationship between the
homotopy classes
of irreducible admissible smooth representations of
G
and of simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules.

This paper is concerned with the Hecke side of that question, i.e. we wish to study the simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules viewed as objects of the homotopy category
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
of
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
. A first step towards this was given by Koziol in
[
19
]
where the simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules of finite projective dimension were classified. When rephrased in our language, this gives a classification of the simple modules which are isomorphic to zero in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
. In particular, it was shown that the simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules are generically never zero in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Our main goal in this work is to treat the harder problem of determining whether two nonisomorphic simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules of infinite projective dimension can become isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
. If
M
denotes a standard Levi subgroup of
G
with associated Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
then a key ingredient in Abe’s construction of the simple modules in
[
1
]
is the
parabolic induction
functor
{\operatorname{Ind}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}:{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
. We therefore first aim to study the properties of
{\operatorname{Ind}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}
in the language of Hovey’s Gorenstein projective model structure. This allows us to essentially reduce our question to the case of supersingular modules. In many situations of interest, we are then able to obtain a rather complete understanding of the situation (cf.
B
&
C
below for details).

1.2.
Main results

We first show that the parabolic induction functor induces decompositions of
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
via various recollements. To state this more precisely, we note that
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
has the same class of objects as
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
, being a localisation of it, and that it was proved by Hovey to be canonically a triangulated category. Fixing a finite collection
{\mathcal{M}}
of proper standard Levi subgroups of
G
, we may therefore let
{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
be the thick subcategory of
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
generated by the essential images of
{\operatorname{Ind}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}
for all
M\in{\mathcal{M}}
. When
{\mathcal{M}}
equals the set of all proper standard Levi subgroups of
G
, we simply write
{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathrm{all}}
for this thick subcategory.

Also, associated to
G
is a group
\Omega
which is equal to the length zero elements in the Iwahori-Weyl group
W=N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I^{\prime})
(where
I^{\prime}
is the
Iwahori
subgroup of
G
with pro-
p
radical
I
). This group
\Omega
is known to be a finitely generated abelian group, and we denote by
\Omega_{\text{tor}}
its (finite) torsion-subgroup. With this notation established, our first main result then says:

Theorem A
.

(i)

The inclusion
i^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}:{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
is part of a recollement

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathcal{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ell^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{r^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}}

of triangulated categories, where
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathcal{M}}={\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})/{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

(ii)

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed, that the root system of
\mathbb{G}
is irreducible and that
p\nmid\left|\Omega_{\text{tor}}\right|
. Suppose that
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
has finite length. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

{\mathfrak{m}}
lies in the essential image of
\ell^{G}_{\mathrm{all}}

(b)

{\mathfrak{m}}
lies in the essential image of
r^{G}_{\mathrm{all}}

(c)

the inclusion
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathrm{ss}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{m}}
is an isomorphism in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
, where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathrm{ss}}
denotes the largest supersingular submodule of
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

This is proved in
3.13
and
3.31
. Roughly speaking, part (i) of
A
says that the triangulated category
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
can be decomposed into two orthogonal thick subcategories, one equal to
{\mathcal{S}}^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
and the other one equal to
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathcal{M}}
, where the latter category is embedded fully faithfully into
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
via either one of the functors
\ell^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
or
r^{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
(cf.
3.9
and the discussion following it for details). We point out that while the proof of (i) is mostly formal, it however relies on the fact that parabolic induction is fully faithful (cf.
2.5
), which appears to be a new result.

We also point out that the recollement in
A
(i) can be entirely reconstructed from various new model structures on
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
. This is proved in the more general context of a recollement in the homotopy category of an abelian model category, see
4.2
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.9
for the details.

The proof of part (ii) of
A
relies on Abe’s classification of the simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules in
[
1
]
, on Koziol’s results on the projective dimension of simple modules from
[
19
]
, and on a lift to the homotopy level of a computation of Abe of the image of the simple modules under the adjoints of the parabolic induction functor (cf.
3.27
). As a consequence of the latter computation, we also obtain a new proof of a result of Koziol and reduce the study of isomorphism classes of simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
to the case of simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, cf.
3.28
.

Having established this, the next step is to determine when two simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules can become isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Here the main difficulty is that computing the Hom spaces in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
a priori requires to compute explicit Gorenstein projective replacements in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Instead, inspired by the arguments of Koziol in
[
19
]
and by the functorial resolutions of Ollivier-Schneider
[
25
]
, we use Bruhat-Tits theory. If
F
denotes a face of the Bruhat-Tits building contained in the closure of a chosen chamber
C
, then there is an associated subalgebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
. These subalgebras together generate a Gorenstein subalgebra
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
called the
affine Hecke algebra
. The simple supersingular Hecke modules were classified by Vignéras, cf.
[
32
]
, and a key input in this classification are the so-called supersingular characters of
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
. Our main insight is to consider the ‘diagonal’ functor

\Delta=({\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{F}})_{F}:{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})\to\prod_{F\subseteq\overline{C}}{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{F})

which is right Quillen and in fact determines the model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})
via a right transfer property, cf.
5.6
. This indicates that we should in principle be able to do computations in the product category
\prod_{F\subseteq\overline{C}}{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}_{F})
instead of
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})
. The advantage here is that there is no need to compute replacements in order to compute Hom spaces in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}_{F})
for any
F
, because
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
is known to be self-injective and this implies that every
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
-module is Gorenstein projective.

More specifically, we have that
\Delta
lifts to a functor
{\operatorname{Ho}}(\Delta):{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})\to\prod_{F\subseteq\overline{C}}{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}}_{F})
. One major technical input in this paper is the computation of the image under
{\operatorname{Ho}}(\Delta)
of the Hom space in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})
between two supersingular characters. Roughly, one gets that this image is always zero unless the root system is of type
\Phi_{1}\times\Phi_{2}
, where
\Phi_{1}
is irreducible of rank 2 and
\Phi_{2}
is a product of
A_{1}
’s, and the two characters are of a very explicit form which we will denote here by
\chi_{1}^{\text{special}}
and
\chi_{2}^{\text{special}}
, see
5.8
for the details. In order to go from supersingular characters of
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
to simple supersingular modules, we then analyse the actions of the group
\Omega
on these characters and on the Hom spaces between these characters. In particular, given a supersingular character
\chi
we denote by
\Omega_{\chi}
its stabiliser in
\Omega
. We also note that
\Omega
acts on the faces
F
contained in
\overline{C}
, and we denote the stabiliser of a face
F
by
\Omega_{F}
. By combining this analysis of the
\Omega
-action on Hom spaces in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})
, cf.
5.22
, with the above computation of the image of Hom spaces under
{\operatorname{Ho}}(\Delta)
, we obtain the following:

Theorem B
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed and let
{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
be two simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules of infinite projective dimension.

(i)

Suppose that
\mathbb{G}
is semisimple and simply-connected. Then
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
if and only if
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

(ii)

Suppose that if one of
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathfrak{m}})
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime})
contains
\chi_{1}^{\text{special}}
as a submodule then the other one does not contain
\chi_{2}^{\text{special}}
. Furthermore, if we fix a supersingular character
\chi
occuring in
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathfrak{m}})
, assume that there exists a face
F\subseteq\overline{C}
such that
\Omega_{\chi}\subseteq\Omega_{F}
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{F}}(\chi)
is not projective. Then
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
if and only if
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

The above is a combination of
5.17
and
5.26
. We then use these results to give a complete classification of the isomorphism classes of simple supersingular modules in the situation where
G
is a product of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
’s. Roughly, we then get that two nonisomorphic simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules almost never become isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
and when they do it is only in a restricted family of cases. In what follows, we denote by
\widetilde{\Omega}
the lift of
\Omega
to
\widetilde{W}=N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I)
. This group embeds canonically as a subgroup of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\times}
.

Theorem C

(
5.35
)
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed and suppose that
G
is a product of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
’s. Let
{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
be two simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules of infinite projective dimension.

(i)

If
G
does not have root system of type
A_{2}\times A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{1}
(possibly empty product of
A_{1}
’s), then
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
if and only if
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

(ii)

If
G
does have root system of type
A_{2}\times A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{1}
(possibly empty product of
A_{1}
’s), then
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
if and only if either
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
or, up to swapping
{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
, we have that
\chi_{1}^{\text{special}}
and
\chi_{2}^{\text{special}}
occur as submodules of
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathfrak{m}})
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime})
respectively and
{\mathfrak{m}}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
as representations of
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

In the case
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, we note in particular that
C
says that two simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules are isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
if and only if they are isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
, except if
n=3
. As the
n
-dimensional simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules are in bijection with certain irreducible
n
-dimensional Galois representations by the work of Grosse-Klönne
[
12
]
, our results show that the modules appearing on the Hecke side of this bijection are generically still non-isomorphic in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

1.3.
Organisation of the paper

In §2.1-2.3 we recall background material on pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebras, parabolic induction and simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, and we prove that parabolic induction is fully faithful. In §2.4-2.5 we recall the notion of an abelian model category, left/right transfers of model structures and the definition of Hovey’s Gorenstein projective model structure, and we prove some general preparatory lemmas.

In §3.1-3.2, we study the properties of the functor of parabolic induction in the language of model categories, culminating with the proof of
A
(i). We only use the fact that parabolic induction is fully faithful, has both a left and a right adjoint, and its left adjoint is exact. Consequently, §3.1 is written in a higher level of generality (cf.
3.1
). In §3.3, we study the relation between the recollement of
A
(i) and supersingular modules and prove
A
(ii).

In §4.1, we investigate recollements in the homotopy category of an abelian model category
{\mathcal{A}}
. We show that if
{\mathcal{A}}
is equipped with both a projective and injective model structure with the same class of trivial objects, then any recollement in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})
can be reconstructed from new model structures on
{\mathcal{A}}
. In the case of Hecke modules, we show that two of these new model structures can be right transferred to
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
. In §4.2, we show that these new model structures are Bousfield localisations of the original model structures on
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Finally, in §5 we compute the isomorphism classes of simple supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. After recalling the necessary background on the affine Hecke algebra and supersingular characters in §5.1, we study the image of the Hom space between two supersingular characters under the functor
{\operatorname{Ho}}(\Delta)
in §5.2, proving
B
(i). In §5.3, we study the action of
\Omega
on the Hom spaces in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}})
, proving
B
(ii). We finish in §5.4 by analysing the case where
G
is a product of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
’s and prove
C
.

Smooth modular representation theory of
p
-adic reductive groups
, project number 435414187. The author is also a member of the DFG Research Training Group 2553
‘Symmetries and Classifying Spaces – Analytic, Arithmetic and Derived’
. The financial support of the DFG is gratefully acknowledged. We thank an anonymous referee for providing detailed feedback and for making several helpful suggestions.

1.5.
Notation and conventions

A class of objects of a category
{\mathcal{C}}
will usually be identified with the corresponding full subcategory. If we denote an adjunction by
F:{\mathcal{C}}\rightleftarrows{\mathcal{D}}:U
then
F
is always assumed to be left adjoint to
U
. The unit (resp. the counit) of an adjunction will always be denoted by
\eta
(resp.
\varepsilon
). When such an adjunction is a Quillen adjunction between model categories, we denote by
\mathbf{L}F:{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{C}})\rightleftarrows{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{D}}):\mathbf{R}U
the total derived adjunction between the homotopy categories. Given a functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
, we denote by
{\operatorname{Im}}(F)
its essential image. We also say that
F

preserves
(resp.
reflects
) a property (P) if
F*
(resp.
*
) has property (P) whenever
*
(resp.
F*
) does.

For any unital ring
R
we denote by
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
the category of right
R
-modules. Given
{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
we write
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
for the set of
R
-linear maps from
{\mathfrak{m}}
to
\mathfrak{n}
, and we denote by
{\operatorname{pd}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}})
and
{\operatorname{id}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}})
the projective and injective dimensions of
{\mathfrak{m}}
, respectively. For any topological group
J
and field
k
, we denote by
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(J)
the category of
k
-linear smooth representations of
J
, i.e. the category of all
k
-vector spaces
V
carrying a
k
-linear action of
J
such that the stabiliser of any
v\in V
is open in
J
. The representation
V=k
equipped with the trivial
J
-action will be denoted by
\mathbf{1}\in{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(J)
. Finally, for any
V\in{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(J)
and any subgroup
J^{\prime}\leq J
we write
V^{J^{\prime}}
for the vector subspace of
J^{\prime}
-invariants, i.e. of
v\in V
such that
j\cdot v=v
for all
j\in J^{\prime}
.

The conventions for pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebras across the literature can differ depending on the authors. Our previous work
[
9
]
as well as
[
25
]
define
{\mathcal{H}}={\operatorname{End}}_{G}({\operatorname{ind}}_{I}^{G}(\mathbf{1}))^{\text{op}}
and work with
left

{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, while the literature on parabolic induction for Hecke modules defines
{\mathcal{H}}={\operatorname{End}}_{G}({\operatorname{ind}}_{I}^{G}(\mathbf{1}))
and works with
right

{\mathcal{H}}
-modules. Since this paper relies on many existing computations with parabolic induction, and in order to avoid confusions with the notions of positive/negative elements, we will stick with the latter convention and work with right
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules.

2.
Reminders on pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebras and model structures

2.1.
The Bruhat-Tits building and Weyl groups

Let
p
be a prime and let
\mathfrak{F}
be a nonarchimedean local field of residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of characteristic
p
. Let
G=\mathbb{G}(\mathfrak{F})
denote the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-rational points of a split connected reductive algebraic group defined over
\mathfrak{F}
. We fix a maximal split
\mathfrak{F}
-torus
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbb{G}
and let
\mathbb{C}
denote the connected component of the centre of
\mathbb{G}
. We denote by
T=\mathbb{T}(\mathfrak{F})
and
Z=\mathbb{C}(\mathfrak{F})
the corresponding groups of
\mathfrak{F}
-rational points. Let
r_{ss}
, resp.
r_{Z}
, denote the semisimple rank of
G
, resp. the rank of
Z
.

We let
\mathscr{X}
denote the semisimple Bruhat-Tits building of
G
. In the standard apartment
X_{*}(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{C})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{R}
of
\mathscr{X}
, we fix a chamber
C
and a hyperspecial vertex
x_{0}
such that
x_{0}\in\overline{C}
. Given a face
F
in
\mathscr{X}
, we let
P_{F}
denote the parahoric subgroup of
G
associated to
F
and
P_{F}^{\dagger}
the stabiliser in
G
of the face
F
. The group
P_{F}
has a pro-
p
radical which we denote by
I_{F}
. In particular, we set
I=I_{C}
and
I^{\prime}=P_{C}
which are called a pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup and Iwahori subgroup of
G
, respectively.

The chamber
C
determines a set
\Phi^{+}
of positive roots of the root system
\Phi\subseteq X^{*}(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{C})
of
(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{T})
. We will view elements of
\Phi
as characters of
T
. If
\Phi^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
\Phi
, we write
\mathrm{rk}(\Phi^{\prime})
to denote its rank. We let
\Pi
denote the basis of
\Phi
defined by
\Phi^{+}
, and
B=T\ltimes U
the Borel subgroup containing
T
defined by
\Phi^{+}
, where
U
is the unipotent radical of
B
. A
standard parabolic subgroup

P=M\ltimes N
is any parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
B
. The corresponding Levi subgroup
M
will also be called
standard
. Given a standard parabolic subgroup
P=MN
, we let
\Pi_{M}
(resp.
\Phi_{M}
, resp.
\Phi_{M}^{+}
) be the corresponding simple roots (resp. root system, resp. positive roots) defined by
M
. We will sometimes also write
\Pi_{P}
to denote this same set of simple roots.

Now let
W_{0}=N_{G}(T)/T
be the finite Weyl group of
G
, with length function
\ell:W_{0}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
defined with respect to
\Pi
. Moreover we denote by

W=N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I^{\prime})\cong\Lambda\rtimes W_{0}

the extended Weyl group of
(G,T)
, where
\Lambda:=T/(T\cap I^{\prime})
. The action of
G
on
\mathscr{X}
restricts to an action of
W
on the standard apartment by affine automorphisms. We let

\langle-,-\rangle:X^{*}(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{C})\times X_{*}(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{C})\to{\mathbb{Z}}

denote the natural perfect pairing. The action of
T
on the standard apartment is then given by translation via
\nu
, where
\nu:T\to X_{*}(\mathbb{T}/\mathbb{C})
is the group homomorphism characterised by

\langle\alpha,\nu(t)\rangle=-{\operatorname{val}}(\alpha(t))

for all
\alpha\in\Phi
and all
t\in T
. Here
{\operatorname{val}}:\mathfrak{F}^{\times}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the normalised valuation.

We now set

\widetilde{\Lambda}:=T/(T\cap I)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{W}:=N_{G}(T)/(T\cap I).

Note that the map
\nu
descends to
\Lambda
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. The quotient
(T\cap I^{\prime})/(T\cap I)
identifies with the group of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational points of
\mathbb{T}
, which we denote by
T(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. So we see that
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is an extension of
\Lambda
by
T(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. For any standard Levi subgroup
M
, we let
W_{0,M}\subset W_{0}
be the corresponding finite Weyl group. We then denote by
W_{M}
the preimage of
W_{0,M}
under the natural projection
W\twoheadrightarrow W_{0}
. Given a subset
X\subseteq W
we write
\widetilde{X}
for its pre-image under the projection
\widetilde{W}\twoheadrightarrow W
. Also, given any
w\in W_{0}
we fix a lift
\hat{w}\in\widetilde{W}
. Note that the length function
\ell
extends to both
W
and
\widetilde{W}
.

By the identification
W\cong\Lambda\rtimes W_{0}
, we may (and indeed always will) view
W_{0}
as a subgroup of
W
. Moreover, any element
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
may be written as
\lambda\hat{\overline{w}}
, where
\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
and
\overline{w}
is the image of
\tilde{w}
in
W_{0}
under the projection. We also define

W_{0}^{M}:=\{w\in W_{0}\mid w(\Phi^{+}_{M})\subset\Phi^{+}\}.

By the Bruhat decomposition,

(2.1)

G=\coprod_{\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}}I\tilde{w}I,\quad M=\coprod_{\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{M}}I_{M}\tilde{w}I_{M}

where
I_{M}:=I\cap M
is a pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup of
M
.

Let
\Omega=\{w\in W\mid\ell(w)=0\}
. Then we have a decomposition
W=W_{\text{aff}}\rtimes\Omega
where
W_{\text{aff}}
denotes the affine Weyl group, generated by the set
S
of simple affine reflections fixing the walls of
C
. Moreover, if we let
\Pi_{\operatorname{aff}}=\Pi\cup\{(\alpha_{1},1),\ldots,(\alpha_{r},1)\}\subseteq\Phi^{+}_{\operatorname{aff}}
where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}
range over the highest roots in each irreducible component of the root system
\Phi
, then the elements of
S
are all of the form
s_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Pi_{\operatorname{aff}}
(cf.
[
25
, §4.3]
). Given any affine root
\alpha
of the form
\alpha(t)=\beta(t)+\lambda
with
\beta\in\Phi
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
t\in T
, we define
\alpha^{\vee}:=\beta^{\vee}
. For any given
s=s_{\alpha}\in S
, we let the subtorus
T_{s}\subseteq T
denote the
\mathfrak{F}
-rational points of the algebraic group image of the cocharacter
\alpha^{\vee}
. Its group of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational points
T_{s}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is a subgroup of
T(\mathbb{F}_{q})
isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. Now, given an
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
-valued character
\xi
of
T(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we let

(2.2)

S_{\xi}:=\{s\in S\mid\xi(t)=1\text{ for all }t\in T_{s}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\}.

The group
\Omega
is a finitely generated abelian group and we write
\Omega_{\text{tor}}
for its (finite) torsion subgroup.

To each face
F\subseteq\overline{C}
we associate the subset
S_{F}\subseteq S
of those reflections fixing
F
pointwise and we let
W_{F}
denote the subgroup of
W
generated by
S_{F}
. Note that
S_{C}=\emptyset
. If
\Phi=\sqcup_{i=1}^{r}\Phi_{i}
is the decomposition of
\Phi
into irreducible components and
\Pi=\sqcup_{i=1}^{r}\Pi_{i}
is the corresponding decomposition of the basis, we also have that
S=\sqcup_{i=1}^{r}S_{i}
where, in the above notation,
S_{i}=\{s_{\beta}\mid\beta\in\Pi_{i}\}\cup\{s_{(\alpha_{i},1)}\}
. Then the map

(2.3)

\displaystyle\{\text{faces $F$ with $F\subseteq\overline{C}$}\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{S^{\prime}\subseteq S\mid\text{$S^{\prime}\cap S_{i}\neq S_{i}$ for every $1\leq i\leq r$}\}

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\longmapsto S_{F}

is a bijection (cf.
[
8
, §1.3.5]
). Moreover, this bijection is order-reversing in the sense that
F^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{F}
if and only if
S_{F}\subseteq S_{F^{\prime}}
.

2.2.
Pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebras

Let
k
denote a field of characteristic
p
, and set

{\mathbb{X}}:={\operatorname{ind}}_{I}^{G}(\mathbf{1})=\{f:I\backslash G\to k\mid f\text{ has finite support}\}\in{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)

with
G
-action given by right translation. The representation
{\mathbb{X}}
is a left module over its endomorphism ring

{\mathcal{H}}:={\operatorname{End}}_{G}({\mathbb{X}}),

the so-called pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebra of
G
with respect to
I
over
k
. By Frobenius reciprocity,
{\mathcal{H}}
can be identified with the convolution
k
-algebra

{\mathcal{H}}={\operatorname{Hom}}_{G}({\mathbb{X}},{\mathbb{X}})\cong{\mathbb{X}}^{I}={\operatorname{ind}}_{I}^{G}(\mathbf{1})^{I}=k[I\backslash G/I]

of
I
-biinvariant compactly supported maps
G\to k
. This construction has analogues for various subgroups of
G
, which we briefly recall now.

For any face
F
of
\mathscr{X}
, we set

{\mathbb{X}}_{F}={\operatorname{ind}}_{I_{C(F)}}^{P_{F}}(\mathbf{1})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{X}}_{F}^{\dagger}={\operatorname{ind}}_{I_{C(F)}}^{P_{F}^{\dagger}}(\mathbf{1}),

where
C(F)
is the chamber defined in
[
18
, Lemma 1.3]
, and the corresponding Hecke algebras are then defined analogously as

{\mathcal{H}}_{F}={\operatorname{End}}_{P_{F}}({\mathbb{X}}_{F})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{F}^{\dagger}={\operatorname{End}}_{P_{F}^{\dagger}}({\mathbb{X}}_{F}^{\dagger}).

When
F\subseteq\overline{C}
then
C(F)=C
,
I_{C(F)}=I
and so extending functions by zero gives embeddings of
{\mathbb{X}}_{F}
and
{\mathbb{X}}_{F}^{\dagger}
into
{\mathbb{X}}
which are
P_{F}
-equivariant and
P_{F}^{\dagger}
-equivariant respectively. The algebras
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}^{\dagger}
can then be naturally identified with subalgebras of
{\mathcal{H}}
by viewing them as
I
-biinvariant functions on
P_{F}
and
P_{F}^{\dagger}
respectively and extending functions by zero. We then have that
{\mathcal{H}}
is free over
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}^{\dagger}
, which itself is also free over
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
(cf.
[
25
, Lemma 4.20 & Proposition 4.21]
).

We also let
G_{\operatorname{aff}}
be the subgroup of
G
generated by all the parahoric subgroups
P_{F}
for
F
varying among all the faces of
\mathscr{X}
. We can then also construct a
G_{\operatorname{aff}}
-representation
{\mathbb{X}}_{\operatorname{aff}}={\operatorname{ind}}_{I}^{G_{\operatorname{aff}}}(\mathbf{1})
and an associated Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}={\operatorname{End}}_{G_{\operatorname{aff}}}({\mathbb{X}}_{\operatorname{aff}})
. Via extension by zero, we can once again identify
{\mathbb{X}}_{\operatorname{aff}}
with a
G_{\operatorname{aff}}
-subrepresentation of
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
with a
k
-subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}
. Viewing all the above Hecke algebras as subalgebras of
{\mathcal{H}}
, we note that
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
contains all the
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
for
F\subseteq\overline{C}
and is in fact generated by them as an algebra (cf. the proof of
[
18
, Lemma 1.4]
). Furthermore, for any
F\subseteq\overline{C}
we have that
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
is free as both a left and right
{\mathcal{H}}_{F}
-module.

For any standard Levi subgroup
M
, we also let
{\mathbb{X}}_{M}:={\operatorname{ind}}_{I_{M}}^{M}(\mathbf{1})
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}:={\operatorname{End}}_{M}({\mathbb{X}}_{M})
. By Frobenius reciprocity,
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
can again be identified with the convolution
k
-algebra of
I_{M}
-biinvariant compactly supported maps
M\to k
. Note however that
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
is
not
a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}
in general. By Frobenius reciprocity again, we have a functor
U_{M}:{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(M)\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})
defined by
U_{M}(V):={\operatorname{Hom}}_{M}({\mathbb{X}}_{M},V)\cong V^{I_{M}}
for any
V\in{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(M)
.

For any
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{M}
, we set
T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}:=I_{M}\tilde{w}I_{M}\in k[I_{M}\backslash M/I_{M}]={\mathcal{H}}_{M}
, the characteristic function of the double coset
I_{M}\tilde{w}I_{M}
. When
M=G
, we drop the superscript and write
T_{\tilde{w}}
. The ring structure of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
is determined by the braid relation

T^{M}_{\tilde{w}\tilde{w}^{\prime}}=T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}T^{M}_{\tilde{w}^{\prime}}\quad\text{if}\quad\ell_{M}(\tilde{w}\tilde{w}^{\prime})=\ell_{M}(\tilde{w})+\ell_{M}(\tilde{w}^{\prime}),

and a certain quadratic relation, cf. (
5.1
) later. By the Bruhat decomposition (
2.1
), the set
\{T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}\mid\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}\}
is a
k
-basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
. There are also certain dual elements
T^{M,*}_{\tilde{w}}
which form another
k
-basis of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
, cf. §4.8, eq. (4.11) in
loc. cit
. In addition, the subalgebra
{\mathcal{H}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
also has a
k
-basis given by the set
\{T_{\tilde{w}}\mid\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{\operatorname{aff}}\}
.

Recall from
[
1
, §4.1]
that for
w\in W_{0,M}
and
\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
, the element
\lambda\hat{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{M}
is called
M
-positive
(resp.
M
-negative
) if
\langle\alpha,\nu(\lambda)\rangle\leq 0
(resp.
\langle\alpha,\nu(\lambda)\rangle\geq 0
) for every
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{M}^{+}
. The
k
-span of the
T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}
with
M
-positive (resp.
M
-negative)
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
forms a
k
-subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
which we denote by
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{+}
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}
). By Lemma 4.6 of
loc. cit.
(see also
[
29
, Theorem 1.4(iii)]
) we have injective algebra homomorphisms

(2.4)

j_{M}^{+}:{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{+}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}\quad\text{and}\quad j_{M}^{-}:{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}

given respectively by
j_{M}^{+}(T^{M}_{\tilde{w}})=T_{\tilde{w}}
and
j_{M}^{-}(T^{M,*}_{\tilde{w}})=T^{*}_{\tilde{w}}
for all
M
-positive (resp.
M
-negative)
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{M}
. Now fix
\lambda_{0}^{+},\lambda_{0}^{-}\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
such that they are in the centre of
\widetilde{W}_{M}
and
\langle\alpha,\nu(\lambda_{0}^{+})\rangle<0
(resp.
\langle\alpha,\nu(\lambda_{0}^{-})\rangle>0
) for every
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{M}^{+}
. We will need the following:

Proposition 2.1

(
[
29
, Theorem 1.4(ii)]
)
.

For any
\lambda_{0}^{+},\lambda_{0}^{-}\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
chosen as above, the elements
T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{\pm}}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{\pm}
are central and non-zero divisors, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
is the Ore localisation of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{\pm}
at
T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{\pm}}
.

We finally need to introduce twists by the longest element
w_{0}\in W_{0}
of the finite Weyl group. Given a standard parabolic
P
with Levi subgroup
M
, let
w_{0}^{M}=w_{0}w_{0,M}
where
w_{0,M}
denotes the longest element in
W_{0,M}
. Then we let
w_{0}(M)
denote the standard Levi subgroup determined by
W_{0,w_{0}(M)}=w_{0}W_{0,M}w_{0}
, and we let
w_{0}(P)
be the standard parabolic whose Levi subgroup is
w_{0}(M)
. Lastly, we fix a lift
\hat{w}_{0}^{M}\in\widetilde{W}
of
w_{0}^{M}
.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
29
, Proposition 2.20]
)
.

The
k
-linear map

{\mathcal{H}}_{M}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)},\quad\quad T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}\mapsto T^{w_{0}(M)}_{\hat{w}_{0}^{M}\tilde{w}(\hat{w}_{0}^{M})^{-1}}\quad\text{for }\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}_{M},

is an isomorphism of
k
-algebras. This induces an exact equivalence of categories

\iota_{0}^{M}:{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)})

such that
\iota_{0}^{M}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)}
.

The above satisfies a transitivity property as follows. If
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
are standard parabolics with Levi subgroups
M\subseteq M^{\prime}
, define
w_{M^{\prime}}^{M}:=w_{0,M^{\prime}}w_{0,M}
. Then as above we have an equivalence of categories
\iota_{M^{\prime}}^{M}:{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0,M^{\prime}}(M)})
induced by the isomorphism
T^{M}_{\tilde{w}}\mapsto T^{w_{0,M^{\prime}}(M)}_{\hat{w}_{M^{\prime}}^{M}\tilde{w}(\hat{w}_{M^{\prime}}^{M})^{-1}}
. Furthermore, the map
T^{w_{0,M^{\prime}}(M)}_{\tilde{w}}\mapsto T^{w_{0}(M)}_{\hat{w}_{0}^{M^{\prime}}\tilde{w}(\hat{w}_{0}^{M^{\prime}})^{-1}}
also defines an isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0,M^{\prime}}(M)}\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)}
which induces an equivalence
\iota_{0,M^{\prime}}^{M}:{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0,M^{\prime}}(M)})\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)})
. Then the equality

(2.5)

\iota_{0}^{M}=\iota_{0,M^{\prime}}^{M}\circ\iota_{M^{\prime}}^{M}

holds, cf.
[
29
, Proposition 2.21]
.

2.3.
Parabolic induction and simple modules

We recall the construction of the parabolic induction and coinduction functors from
[
29
, §3]
. We fix a standard Levi subgroup
M
of
G
. By (
2.4
) we may view the algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
as an
({\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{+},{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-})
-bimodule via the maps
j_{M}^{+}
and
j_{M}^{-}
respectively. Thus we may define two functors
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})\to{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
by setting

{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}}):={\mathfrak{m}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{+}}{\mathcal{H}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}}):={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathfrak{m}})

for any
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})
. The functors
{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
are called
parabolic induction
and
parabolic coinduction
, respectively.

By the tensor-hom adjunction, we see that
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
has left adjoint
{\operatorname{Loc}}_{M}:=(-)\otimes_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}}{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
and
{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
has right adjoint
{\mathcal{R}^{G}_{M}}:={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{+}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M},-)
. Note that since
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
is the Ore localisation of
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}
at
T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{-}}
, the functor
{\operatorname{Loc}}_{M}
is the corresponding localisation functor and hence is exact. The counit of the adjoint pair
({\operatorname{Loc}}_{M},{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}})
is given as follows. For
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})
, we have a natural map
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}})={\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to{\mathfrak{m}}
by evaluating at
1
. Localising at
T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{-}}
this gives the counit

(2.6)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{{\mathfrak{m}}}:{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}^{-}}{\mathcal{H}}_{M}

\displaystyle\to{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes(T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{-}})^{-n}

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi(1)\cdot(T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{-}})^{-n}

The main properties of these functors that we’ll need are summarised below:

Theorem 2.3

(
[
29
, 1.6-1.10]
)
.

Let
M
be a standard Levi subgroup of
G
. Then:

(i)

there are natural isomorphisms

{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\cong{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\circ\iota_{0}^{M}\quad\text{and}\quad{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\cong{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{w_{0}(M)}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\circ\iota_{0}^{M},

where
\iota_{0}^{M}
is the equivalence from
2.2
; and

(ii)

parabolic induction and coinduction are transitive and have both a left and a right adjoint. In particular they are exact functors.

Note that the left (resp. right) adjoint of
{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
(resp.
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
) can be described explicitly, namely it is equal to the composite
\mathcal{L}_{M}^{G}:=\iota_{0}^{w_{0}(M)}\circ{\operatorname{Loc}}_{w_{0}(M)}
(resp.
\iota_{0}^{w_{0}(M)}\circ\mathcal{R}^{G}_{w_{0}(M)}
), cf.
[
29
, Theorem 1.9]
.

We will use the next general categorical fact several times, so we record it here:

Lemma 2.4

(
[
17
, Proposition 1.5.6]
)
.

Suppose
F:{\mathcal{C}}\rightleftarrows{\mathcal{D}}:U
is an adjunction, and denote by
\eta
and
\varepsilon
the unit and counit respectively. Then
F
(resp.
U
) is fully faithful if and only if
\eta
(resp.
\varepsilon
) is an isomorphism.

The next result is, to the best of our knowledge, not explicitly available anywhere in the literature. Its proof was however inspired by the arguments used in the proof of
[
19
, Lemma 8.1]
. We note that it also answers the question asked in the last paragraph of
[
29
, §1]
, in the case where the coefficient ring is a field of characteristic
p
.

Proposition 2.5
.

For any standard parabolic subgroup
M
of
G
, the functors
{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
are fully faithful.

Proof.

It suffices to show the result for coinduction since the two functors differ by a twist. By
2.4
we must show that the counit
\varepsilon_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is an isomorphism for all
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})
. By
[
1
, Lemma 4.10]
(see also
[
29
, Lemma 3.9]
), the map
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi(T_{\hat{v}}))_{v\in W_{0}^{M}}
gives a
k
-linear isomorphism
{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}({\mathfrak{m}})\cong\bigoplus_{v\in W_{0}^{M}}{\mathfrak{m}}
, where
\hat{v}
is a fixed lift of
v
to
\widetilde{W}
(and by convention we impose for
v=1
that
\hat{v}=1_{\widetilde{W}}
). Under this identification and from (
2.6
), we see that
\varepsilon_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is the localisation of the projection onto the
v=1
summand.

Now, by
[
1
, Proposition 4.12]
(whose statement assumes
k
to be algebraically closed but whose proof works for arbitrary fields of characteristic
p
) and
[
29
, Lemma 2.18]
, the action of
T_{\lambda_{0}^{-}}
on the summand corresponding to
v\in W_{0}^{M}
is zero if
\hat{v}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-})
is not
M
-negative, and the action on the
v=1
component agrees with the action of
T^{M}_{\lambda_{0}^{-}}
on
{\mathfrak{m}}
. Hence, from the definition of
\varepsilon_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
, we’ll be done if we show that
\hat{v}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-})
is not
M
-negative for
v\neq 1
.

We argue by induction on
\ell(v)
. Pick
\alpha\in\Pi
such that
\ell(s_{\alpha}v)<\ell(v)
. First assume that
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{M}^{+}
, noting that this holds in the base case
\ell(v)=1
by definition of
W_{0}^{M}
. Then, using the fact that
v\in W_{0}^{M}
and that
v(-v^{-1}(\alpha))=-\alpha\in-\Phi^{+}
, we obtain that
-v^{-1}(\alpha)\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{M}^{+}
. Then, we get

\langle-v^{-1}(\alpha),\nu(\hat{v}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-}))\rangle=-\langle\alpha,\nu(\lambda_{0}^{-})\rangle<0

by definition of
\lambda_{0}^{-}
, and so
\hat{v}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-})
is not
M
-negative as required.

Assuming instead that
\alpha\in\Phi_{M}^{+}
, we then have that
(\hat{s_{\alpha}}\hat{v})^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-})=\hat{v}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{-})
since
\lambda_{0}^{-}
is central in
\widetilde{W}_{M}
. But since
\ell(s_{\alpha}v)<\ell(v)
and
v\in W_{0}^{M}
we also have

\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}\mid s_{\alpha}v(\beta)\in-\Phi^{+}\}\subseteq\{\beta\in\Phi^{+}\mid v(\beta)\in-\Phi^{+}\}\subseteq\Phi^{+}\setminus\Phi_{M}^{+},

and thus
s_{\alpha}v\in W_{0}^{M}
. We are then done by the inductive hypothesis.
∎

We now recall the classification of simple Hecke modules. Here we assume that
k
is algebraically closed. The classification was initially stated in terms of parabolic coinduction by Abe
[
1
, Theorem 4.22]
, and was later reworded in terms of parabolic induction (cf.
[
4
, Corollary 4.30]
). We first need to recall the definition of a supersingular
{\mathcal{H}}
-module as follows. Recall from
[
30
, §5]
that for a choice of spherical orientation
o
, i.e. of a closed Weyl chamber of
W_{0}
, we can associate a basis
\{E_{o}(\tilde{w})\}_{\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
with the property that
E_{o}(\tilde{w})\in T_{\tilde{w}}+\sum_{\tilde{v}<\tilde{w}}kT_{\tilde{v}}
(where
\tilde{v}<\tilde{w}
refers to the Bruhat order). Now let
\mathcal{O}
be a
\widetilde{W}
-conjugacy class in
\tilde{\Lambda}
, and let

z_{\mathcal{O}}=\sum_{\tilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}}E_{o}(\tilde{\lambda}).

Then this element does not depend on the choice of orientation
o
, and defines an element of the centre of
{\mathcal{H}}
, cf.
[
32
, Theorem 5.1]
. The length of
\tilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}
is independent of the choice of representative
\tilde{\lambda}
, and we denote it by
\ell(\mathcal{O})
.

Definition 2.6
.

An
{\mathcal{H}}
-module
{\mathfrak{m}}
is called
supersingular
if for each
\widetilde{W}
-conjugacy class
\mathcal{O}
in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
with
\ell(\mathcal{O})>0
and for every
x\in{\mathfrak{m}}
, there exists some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
xz_{\mathcal{O}}^{n}=0
. If the integer
n
can be chosen independently of
x
and
\mathcal{O}
, we call
{\mathfrak{m}}

uniformly supersingular
.

Note that the notions of supersingular and uniformly supersingular coincide for
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules of finite length, or more generally for finitely generated modules, and hence in particular for simple modules.

Next, a
standard simple supersingular triple
for
{\mathcal{H}}
is a triple
(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
such that

•

P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi decomposition
P=MN
;

•

\mathfrak{n}
is a simple supersingular module over
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
; and

•

Q
is a parabolic subgroup of
G
with
P\subseteq Q\subseteq P(\mathfrak{n})
, where
P(\mathfrak{n})
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
corresponding to
\Pi(\mathfrak{n}):=\Pi_{M}\cup\Pi_{\mathfrak{n}}\subseteq\Pi
and
\alpha\in\Pi_{\mathfrak{n}}
if and only if both
\langle\Pi_{M},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=0
and
T_{\lambda}^{M}
acts trivially on
\mathfrak{n}
for all
\lambda\in T^{\prime}_{\alpha}
.

In the above, for
\alpha\in\Pi
, we set
T^{\prime}_{\alpha}=T\cap G^{\prime}_{\alpha}
where
G^{\prime}_{\alpha}
denotes the subgroup of
G
generated by the root subgroups corresponding to
\pm\alpha
. Two supersingular triples
(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
and
(P^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
are called equivalent if
P=P^{\prime}
,
Q=Q^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{n}\cong\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
.

Given a supersingular triple
(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
, we define
I_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q),CI_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)\in{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
as follows. Suppose that
Q
has Levi decomposition
Q=LV
. Now assume that
Q^{\prime}
is any parabolic subgroup of
G
with
P\subseteq Q^{\prime}\subseteq P(\mathfrak{n})
and say it has Levi decomposition
Q^{\prime}=L^{\prime}V^{\prime}
. Then
\mathfrak{n}
can be extended uniquely to an
{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}
-module
e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}(\mathfrak{n})
with certain properties, and moreover whenever
P\subseteq Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}\subseteq P(\mathfrak{n})
there is a natural injective
{\mathcal{H}}
-linear map
{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}(\mathfrak{n}))\to{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}(\mathfrak{n}))
(cf.
[
4
, Corollary 3.9 & Proposition 4.5]
). There is also an analogous map for coinduction, cf. the discussion preceding
[
1
, Theorem 4.22]
. We may thus define the modules

(2.7)

I_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q):={\operatorname{coker}}\left(\bigoplus_{Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}\subseteq P(\mathfrak{n})}{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}(\mathfrak{n}))\to{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}(\mathfrak{n}))\right)

and

(2.8)

CI_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q):={\operatorname{coker}}\left(\bigoplus_{Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}\subseteq P(\mathfrak{n})}{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L^{\prime}}}(\mathfrak{n}))\to{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}^{\mathcal{H}}(e_{{\mathcal{H}}_{L}}(\mathfrak{n}))\right).

The classification of simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules states that
(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)\mapsto I_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
and
(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)\mapsto CI_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
define two bijections between the set of equivalence classes of standard simple supersingular triples and the set of isomorphism classes of simple
{\mathcal{H}}
-modules, cf.
loc. cit.
and
[
4
, Corollary 4.30]
. Moreover, the two bijections are related by an
{\mathcal{H}}
-equivariant isomorphism

(2.9)

CI_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)\cong I_{\mathcal{H}}(w_{0}(P),\iota_{0}^{M}(\mathfrak{n}),w_{0}(Q))

cf.
[
4
, Corollary 4.24]
. Finally, a simple module
CI_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
is supersingular if and only if
P=Q=G
(cf.
[
1
, Corollary 4.31]
), and analogously for
I_{\mathcal{H}}(P,\mathfrak{n},Q)
.

2.4.
Recollections on abelian model categories

We briefly recall some facts about model structures on abelian categories. For details on the general theory of model categories, we refer the reader to
[
9
, §1]
and the references therein. Fix a bicomplete abelian category
{\mathcal{A}}
. We will say that a model structure on
{\mathcal{A}}
is an
abelian model structure
if the class of (trivial) fibrations, resp. (trivial) cofibrations, coincides with the class of epimorphisms with (trivially) fibrant kernel, resp. monomorphisms with (trivially) cofibrant cokernel. An abelian category equipped with such a model structure will be called an
abelian model category
.

By work of Hovey, cf.
[
15
]
, constructing abelian model structures on
{\mathcal{A}}
is equivalent to constructing certain cotorsion pairs. Given a pair
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}})
of classes of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
, consider the pair
(^{\perp}{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp})
defined by

{}^{\perp}{\mathcal{F}}=\{X\in{\mathcal{A}}\mid{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)=0\text{ for all }Y\in{\mathcal{F}}\}

and

{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp}=\{Y\in{\mathcal{A}}\mid{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)=0\text{ for all }X\in{\mathcal{C}}\}.

Then we say that
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}})
is a
cotorsion pair
if
{\mathcal{C}}={{}^{\perp}}{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{C}}^{\perp}
(cf.
[
15
, Definition 2.3]
). It is said to have
enough functorial projectives
if for all
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, there is a functorial epimorphism
f:Y\to X
with
Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\ker(f)\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Dually, it is said to have
enough functorial injectives
if for all
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, there is a functorial monomorphism
g:X\to Z
with
Z\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that
{\operatorname{coker}}(g)\in{\mathcal{C}}
. When a cotorsion pair has both enough functorial projectives and enough functorial injectives, then it is said to be
functorially complete
.

Recall that a class of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
is called
thick
if it is closed under direct summands and satisfies the 2-out-of-3 property with respect to short exact sequences. When
{\mathcal{A}}
is equipped with a model structure, we will say that an object
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
is
trivial
if the zero map
0\to X
(or equivalently
X\to 0
) is a weak equivalence. The fundamental result we need is the following.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
15
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a bicomplete abelian category.

(i)

If
{\mathcal{A}}
is an abelian model category, denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{F}}
, resp.
{\mathcal{W}}
) the class of cofibrant (resp. fibrant, resp. trivial) objects. Then
{\mathcal{W}}
is thick, and
({\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{F}})
and
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}})
are functorially complete cotorsion pairs.

(ii)

Conversely, assume that
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
is a triple of classes of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
{\mathcal{W}}
is thick, and
({\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{F}})
and
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}})
are functorially complete cotorsion pairs. Then there is a unique abelian model structure on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
coincide with the classes of cofibrant, fibrant and trivial objects, respectively.

In fact, Hovey proved a more general version of the above theorem which applies to a wider class of model structures on
{\mathcal{A}}
, using the notion of a proper class of short exact sequences, and this has been further generalised to the context of exact categories by Gillespie in
[
11
]
. In this paper, we will however only need to work with abelian model structures. We will sometimes denote an abelian model structure on
{\mathcal{A}}
by the triple
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
as in
2.7
. Note that the homotopy category
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})
of an abelian model category is canonically equivalent to the quotient
{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}/\Gamma
, where
\Gamma={\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
(cf.
[
11
, Proposition 4.4(5)]
).

Given an abelian model structure
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, the completeness of the cotorsion pairs
({\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{F}})
and
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}})
ensures that for any
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
there are short exact sequences
0\to Y\to Q_{c}X\to X\to 0
and
0\to X\to Q_{f}X\to Z\to 0
, with
Q_{c}X\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
Q_{f}X\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
and
Z\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
. This can be done functorially in our setup, but by slight abuse of terminology we will refer to
any
two elements
Q_{c}X
and
Q_{f}X
fitting in short exact sequences as above as choices of
cofibrant replacement
and
fibrant replacement
for
X
, respectively. We will always denote such replacements by
Q_{c}X
and
Q_{f}X
as we did above.

As a final piece of notation, if a functor
{\mathsf{F}}:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
between the underlying categories of two abelian model categories preserves all weak equivalences, it then naturally induces a functor
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}}):{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})\to{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{B}})
given by
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}})(X):=F(X)
for
X\in{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})
. When
{\mathsf{F}}
is Quillen we can identify
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}})
with the total derived functor of
{\mathsf{F}}
under mild assumptions as follows.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
are abelian model categories, and let
{\mathsf{F}}:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
be an exact functor between the underlying abelian categories.

(i)

If
{\mathsf{F}}
preserves trivial objects then it preserves all weak equivalences.

(ii)

Assume that
{\mathsf{F}}
is a (left or right) Quillen functor. If
{\mathsf{F}}
reflects trivial objects then it reflects all weak equivalences.

Furthermore, with the assumption of (i) and if
{\mathsf{F}}
is Quillen, then the total derived functor of
{\mathsf{F}}
is naturally isomorphic to
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
is a morphism in
{\mathcal{A}}
. For (i), assume first that
f
is either a trivial fibration or trivial cofibration. Then in particular it is either a monomorphism with trivial cokernel, or an epimorphism with trivial kernel. Under our assumptions then
{\mathsf{F}}f
is still a monomorphism/epimorphism with trivial cokernel/kernel. It then follows from
[
15
, Lemma 5.8]
that
{\mathsf{F}}f
is a weak equivalence. The case of a general
f
follows immediately by factorising
f
as the composite of a trivial cofibration and a trivial fibration.

For (ii), we assume that
{\mathsf{F}}
is left Quillen (the other case is dual). Factorise
f=gh
where
h
is a trivial cofibration and
g
is a fibration. Then by the 2-out-of-3 property of weak equivalences it suffices to show that if
{\mathsf{F}}g
is a weak equivalence then so is
g
. Since
{\mathsf{F}}
is exact, we may apply
loc. cit.
again to see that
{\mathsf{F}}g
is a weak equivalence precisely when
{\mathsf{F}}(\ker(g))
is trivial. It then follows that
g
is a trivial fibration since
{\mathsf{F}}
reflects trivial objects.

Finally, the total derived functor of
{\mathsf{F}}
is defined to be
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}}\circ Q)
where
Q
is some fibrant or cofibrant replacement functor, depending on whether
{\mathsf{F}}
is right or left Quillen. But by definition, any object is weakly equivalent to its replacement and
{\mathsf{F}}
preserves such weak equivalences under the assumption of (i). Thus
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathsf{F}})
is isomorphic the total derived functor in that case, as required.
∎

An abelian model category
{\mathcal{A}}
is called
hereditary
if the class
{\mathcal{C}}
of cofibrant objects is closed under taking kernels of epimorphisms and if the class
{\mathcal{F}}
of fibrant objects is closed under taking cokernels of monomorphisms. Such an abelian model category is automatically stable (cf.
[
7
, Corollary 1.1.15]
), meaning that its associated homotopy category
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})
is canonically equipped with the structure of a triangulated category. We quickly recall what this triangulated structure is.

•

The shift functor is given by the suspension functor
\Sigma
associated with the model structure. For a cofibrant object
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
, choose a cofibration
j_{X}:X\to W
where
W\in\Gamma={\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
. Then we let
\Sigma(X)
be defined as
{\operatorname{coker}}(j_{X})
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is hereditary, note that
\Sigma(X)\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}
whenever
X\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}
. This construction doesn’t depend on the choice of
j_{X}
up to weak equivalence, and hence this defines an endofunctor of
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})\equiv{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}/\Gamma
which can be shown to be an auto-equivalence. There is also a dually defined loop functor which is naturally isomorphic to
\Sigma^{-1}
. We note that the loop and suspension functors can be defined more generally for any (not necessarily stable) pointed model category, cf.
[
6
, Definition 3.1.2]
, and are then adjoint functors.

•

The distinguished triangles are obtained from the so-called cofiber sequences as in
[
6
, Definition 3.4.1 & Theorem 4.2.1]
. In the context of hereditary abelian model categories, these are given as follows. Suppose that
0\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}Z\to 0
is a short exact sequence in
{\mathcal{A}}
with
X,Y,Z\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Fix
j_{X}:X\to W
as above, so that
W\in{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
and
\Sigma X\cong{\operatorname{coker}}(j_{X})
. Since
{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z,W)=0
we have that
j_{X}
extends along
f
to a map
Y\to W
. Postcomposing with the projection
W\to\Sigma X
, we obtain a map
l:Y\to\Sigma X
such that
l\circ f=0
. Thus
l
descends to a map
h:Z\to\Sigma X
. The distinguished triangles in
{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})
are then, up to isomorphism, the triangles of the form

(2.10)

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\to}}\Sigma X

as above.

Remark 2.9
.

There is a dual notion of fiber sequences which may be used to construct the distinguished triangles as well, cf.
[
6
, Definition 3.4.2, Corollaries 4.4.1 & 4.4.2]
. In our context, those are obtained similarly to the above by using short exact sequences of fibrant objects and the loop functor. That these two constructions give the same triangulated structure on the homotopoy category holds in the general context of stable model categories (cf.
loc. cit.
) but for hereditary abelian model categories this can be also seen directly by applying
[
7
, Lemma 1.4.4]
and its dual.

Recall that a ring
R
is called
Gorenstein
if it is both left and right noetherian and if it has finite selfinjective dimension both as a left and as a right
R
-module. In this case the left and right selfinjective dimensions of
R
coincide (cf.
[
10
]
, Proposition 9.1.8). If
n
denotes their common value then
R
is called an
n
-Gorenstein ring
. We will frequently make use of the fact that if
R
is an
n
-Gorenstein ring then an
R
-module
{\mathfrak{m}}
has finite projective dimension if and only if it has finite injective dimension. In this case both dimensions are bounded above by
n
(cf.
[
10
]
, Theorem 9.1.10). The relevance of this notion for us comes from the following result of Ollivier-Schneider.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
25
, Theorem 0.1]
)
.

The pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
is
n
-Gorenstein where
n\leq r_{\text{ss}}+r_{Z}
.

The bound on the selfinjective dimension of
{\mathcal{H}}
is actually sharp (cf.
[
19
, Remark 8.2]
). The theorem applies generally to the algebras
{\mathcal{H}}_{M}
for any standard Levi subgroup
M
of
G
.

We now recall the construction, due to Hovey, of two abelian model structures on the category
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
for any Gorenstein ring
R
. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an abelian category with enough projectives. An object
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
is called
Gorenstein projective
if there is an acyclic complex

Y=\cdots\longrightarrow Y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{-1}\longrightarrow\cdots

of projective objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
for which
A=Z_{0}Y={\operatorname{Ker}}(d_{0})
and which remains exact upon applying
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\cdot,P)
for any projective object
P
of
{\mathcal{A}}
. When
{\mathcal{A}}={\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
for a Gorenstein ring
R
, then a module
{\mathfrak{m}}
is Gorenstein projective as an object of
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
if and only if
{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{p})=0
for all
i\geq 1
and all projective
R
-modules
\mathfrak{p}
(cf.
[
10
, Corollary 11.5.3]
). This is true if and only if
{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{p})=0
for all
i\geq 1
and all modules
\mathfrak{p}
of finite projective dimension. Then
{\mathfrak{m}}
is Gorenstein projective if and only if there is an acyclic complex
Y
of projectives with
{\mathfrak{m}}=Z_{0}Y
, cf.
[
9
, Remark 3.1]
. There is also dually a notion of
Gorenstein injective
object of
{\mathcal{A}}
, and for
{\mathcal{A}}={\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
these have dual properties to the above (cf.
[
10
, Definition 10.1.1 & Corollary 11.2.2]
)). We denote the classes of Gorenstein projective and Gorenstein injective modules over a ring
R
by
\mathrm{GProj}(R)
and
\mathrm{GInj}(R)
, respectively.

The following fundamental theorem is due to Hovey.

Theorem 2.11

(
[
15
, Theorem 8.6]
)
.

Let
R
be a Gorenstein ring and let
{\mathcal{W}}
denote the class of
R
-modules which have finite projective/injective dimension. Then there is a cofibrantly generated abelian model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
given by the triple
(\mathrm{GProj}(R),{\operatorname{Mod}}(R),{\mathcal{W}})
. There is dually a cofibrantly generated abelian model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
given by the triple
({\operatorname{Mod}}(R),\mathrm{GInj}(R),{\mathcal{W}})
.

We call these the
Gorenstein projective
and
Gorenstein injective
model structures on
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
, respectively. We will write
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)^{\text{GP}}
, resp.
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)^{\text{GI}}
, for the category
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
viewed as an abelian model category with the Gorenstein projective, resp. Gorenstein injective, model structure.

Remark 2.12
.

The identity functor
{\operatorname{id}}:{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)^{\text{GP}}\to{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)^{\text{GI}}
is left Quillen and in fact a Quillen equivalence (cf.
[
15
, bottom of p.583]
). The latter means that if
{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
are such that
{\mathfrak{m}}
is Gorenstein projective and
\mathfrak{n}
is Gorenstein injective, then a map between them is a weak equivalence in one model structure if and only if it is a weak equivalence in the other. But this holds because, more generally, the weak equivalences in both model structures are the same. Indeed, that follows from
2.8
as the trivial objects are the same in either model structure. Even without using the notion of Quillen equivalences, we therefore see that the two model structures have equivalent homotopy categories as these are localisations of
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
with respect to the same collection of weak equivalences. We will always denote this homotopy category by
{\operatorname{Ho}}(R)
.

We record here a particular application of
2.8
which will be useful in order to compare the Gorenstein projective model structures over various Gorenstein rings.

Lemma 2.13
.

Suppose that
A
and
B
are Gorenstein rings, with
A
isomorphic to a subring of
B
. Further, assume that
B
is free as a left
A
-module. Write
F^{B}_{A}:{\operatorname{Mod}}(A)\to{\operatorname{Mod}}(B)
for the functor
{\mathfrak{m}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes_{A}B
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
for its right adjoint given by restriction of scalars. Then the adjunction
F^{B}_{A}\dashv{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
is Quillen with respect to the Gorenstein projective model structure and there are natural isomorphisms
\mathbf{L}F^{B}_{A}\cong{\operatorname{Ho}}(F^{B}_{A})
and
\mathbf{R}{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}\cong{\operatorname{Ho}}({\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A})
.

Proof.

Under our assumptions, both
F^{B}_{A}
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
are exact. We claim that they preserve trivial objects, i.e. modules of finite projective/injective dimension. Indeed,
F^{B}_{A}
and
{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
preserve projective and injective modules, respectively, since they are an adjoint pair and exact. It therefore follows by exactness that
F^{B}_{A}
, resp.
{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
, preserves finite projective resolutions, resp. finite injective resolutions, and thus we get our claim. From all of this we also obtain that
{\operatorname{Res}}^{B}_{A}
preserves epimorphisms, i.e. fibrations, and epimorphisms with kernels of finite projective dimension, i.e. trivial fibrations, and hence is right Quillen as required. Finally, the isomorphism on the total derived functors is immediate from
2.8
.
∎

The following characterisation of the morphisms in the homotopy category of any abelian model category is due to Gillespie.

Proposition 2.14

(
[
11
, Proposition 4.4]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an abelian model category with model structure
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
. Suppose
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Then the abelian group
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,Y)
is isomorphic to the quotient of
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(X,Y)
by the equivalence relation given by
f\sim g
if and only if
f-g
factors through an object of
\Gamma={\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
, if and only if
f-g
factors through an object of
{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
, if and only if
f-g
factors through an object of
{\mathcal{F}}\cap{\mathcal{W}}
.

For a Gorenstein ring
R
and writing
{\mathcal{W}}
for the class of
R
-modules of finite projective dimension, it is shown in
[
15
, Corollary 8.5]
that the classes
\mathrm{GProj}(R)\cap{\mathcal{W}}
and
\mathrm{GInj}(R)\cap{\mathcal{W}}
are that of projective and injective
R
-modules respectively. We thus see from
2.14
that for
{\mathfrak{m}}
a Gorenstein projective module and
\mathfrak{n}
any module, two maps
f,g:{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathfrak{n}
are equal in
{\operatorname{Ho}}(R)
if and only if
f-g
factors through a projective module. Similarly, if
{\mathfrak{m}}
is any module and if
\mathfrak{n}
is Gorenstein injective, then two maps
f,g:{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathfrak{n}
are equal in
{\operatorname{Ho}}(R)
if and only if
f-g
factors through an injective module.

To make our notation simpler, for any two
{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
we will denote the
k
-vector space
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\operatorname{Ho}}(R)}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
by
[{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}]_{R}
. More generally, if
R
and
S
are two Gorenstein rings with
R
isomorphic to a subring of
S
and if
{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(S)
, we will write
[{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}]_{R}
for short instead of
[{\operatorname{Res}}^{S}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}}),{\operatorname{Res}}^{S}_{R}(\mathfrak{n})]_{R}
.

Remark 2.15
.

Suppose that
R
is a Gorenstein ring and
f:{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathfrak{n}
is a map in
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
. Fix a surjection
\theta:\mathfrak{p}\to\mathfrak{n}
where
\mathfrak{p}
is a projective module. Assume that
f
factors as a composite
{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathfrak{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{n}
where
\mathfrak{q}
is a projective module. Since
\mathfrak{q}
is projective, it follows that
g
, and hence
f
, factor through
\theta
. Consequently, we see that the maps
f\in{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
which factor through a projective module are precisely those in the image of
\theta\circ(-):{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{p})\to{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
. Dually, if
\iota:{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathfrak{e}
is a monomorphism into an injective module
\mathfrak{e}
then the maps
f\in{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
which factor through a injective module are precisely those in the image of
(-)\circ\iota:{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{n})\to{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}({\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n})
.

By combining
2.14
and
2.15
, we see that for
{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
with
{\mathfrak{m}}
Gorenstein projective and for
\theta:\mathfrak{p}\to\mathfrak{n}
as above,
[{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{n}]_{R}\cong{\operatorname{coker}}(\theta\circ(-))\cong{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{S}({\mathfrak{m}},\ker(\theta))
. This generalises as follows. We say that an abelian model structure
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
on an abelian category
{\mathcal{A}}
is
projective
, resp.
injective
, if
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{A}}
, resp.
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{A}}
. We note that any projective or injective abelian model structure on
{\mathcal{A}}
is hereditary, cf.
[
7
, Corollary 1.1.12]
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an abelian model category with model structure
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{W}})
, and assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
is either injective or projective. Let
X\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Then we have isomorphisms

{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})}(\Sigma^{-k}X,Y)\cong{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\operatorname{Ho}}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,\Sigma^{k}Y)\cong{\operatorname{Ext}}^{k}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)

of abelian groups for all
k>0
.

Proof.

The arguments given in
[
15
, Theorem 9.3]
for Gorenstein projective/injective model structures carry over mutatis mutandis to this more general situation.
∎

Since the Gorenstein projective, resp. Gorenstein injective, model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
is projective, resp. injective, it is hereditary. In particular, it is stable which is also proved directly in
[
15
, Theorem 9.3]
. Hence we see that the homotopy category
{\operatorname{Ho}}(R)
has the structure of a triangulated category as described earlier. We note that since every object is cofibrant in the Gorenstein injective model structure the triangles (
2.10
) are obtained for
any
short exact sequence in
{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
.

2.5.
Left and right transfers

We now recall the notion of left/right transfer of a model structure along an adjunction. If
F:\mathcal{E}\rightleftarrows{\mathcal{D}}:U
is an adjunction where
\mathcal{E}
is a model category and
{\mathcal{D}}
is a locally small, bicomplete category, there is then a natural candidate for a model structure on
{\mathcal{D}}
making the adjunction Quillen. Namely, one defines fibrations (resp. weak equivalences) in
{\mathcal{D}}
to be the morphisms
f
such that
U(f)
is a fibration (resp. weak equivalence). The cofibrations are then defined as those morphisms which satisfy the left lifting property with respect to the trivial fibrations. This, however, may not in general give a well-defined model structure on
{\mathcal{D}}
. When it does, we call it the
right transfer
of the model structure on
\mathcal{E}
along
U
, and we say that the right transfer exists. In
[
9
, Proposition 4.10]
, it was shown that the right transfer of Hovey’s Gorenstein projective model structure on
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
along the functor
U
of pro-
p
Iwahori invariants exists. This gives a model structure on
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
, called the
I
-Gorenstein projective model structure.

Dually, when
{\mathcal{D}}
is a model category and
\mathcal{E}
is a locally small, bicomplete category, the
left transfer
of the model structure on
{\mathcal{D}}
along
F
, if it exists, is the model structure on
\mathcal{E}
whose cofibrations (resp. weak equivalences) are the morphisms
g
such that
F(g)
is a cofibration (resp. weak equivalence), and the fibrations are defined as those morphisms which satisfy the right lifting property with respect to the trivial cofibrations.

3.
Parabolic induction, recollements and supersingular modules

3.1.
Parabolic induction and recollement: one parabolic

We now wish to study how the parabolic (co)induction functor behaves with respect to Hovey’s Gorenstein projective and Gorenstein injective model structures on
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}}_{M})
and
{\operatorname{Mod}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Since we only know that the Gorenstein projective model structure right transfers to the category
{\operatorname{Rep}}_{k}^{\infty}(G)
, we a priori favour that model structure over the injective one. But as we will see, when it comes to parabolic induction it is more fruitful to consider the Gorenstein injective model structure as well. Since working specifically with Hecke algebras does not simplify the approach, we work in this subsection in the more general situation described below.

Setup 3.1
.

We let
R,S
be two Gorenstein rings. Moreover, we assume the following:

•

there is an adjoint triple of additive functors

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.24309pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.24309pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\operatorname{Mod}}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.48976pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{G}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.92218pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.46945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.69582pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 74.92218pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.92726pt\raise 23.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{L}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 12.17767pt\raise 7.99988pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.56267pt\raise-23.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{R}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 12.17767pt\raise-7.99988pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

meaning that
{\mathsf{L}}
(resp.
{\mathsf{R}}
) is the left (resp. right) adjoint of
{\mathsf{G}}
;

•

the functor
{\mathsf{G}}
is fully faithful, and
{\mathsf{L}}
is exact.

Example 3.2
.

The above setup applies to
S={\mathcal{H}}_{M}
(
M
some standard Levi subgroup),
R={\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathsf{G}}\in\{{\operatorname{Coind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}},{\operatorname{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{M}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\}
along with its two adjoints. The left adjoint is exact in either case because
{\operatorname{Loc}}_{M}
is a localisation functor and the equivalence in
2.2
is also exact. We’ve also seen that parabolic (co)induction is fully faithful in
2.5
.

Note that the functor
{\mathsf{G}}
is automatically exact as it is both a left and a right adjoint. But
{\mathsf{R}}
is only known to be left exact, being a right adjoint. Part (i) of the Lemma below is a generalisation of
[
19
, Lemma 8.1]
to our setup:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(S)
and
\mathfrak{n}\in{\operatorname{Mod}}(R)
. Then:

(i)

{\operatorname{pd}}_{S}({\mathfrak{m}})<\infty
if and only if
{\operatorname{pd}}_{R}({\mathsf{G}}({\mathfrak{m}}))<\infty
;

(ii)

if
